1760. CHARLES SIMMONS stealing 1 barrow, value 50 s.; the goods of Henry Conman; also, 1 truck, value 4 l.; the goods of John Edward Smith: to which he pleaded
GUILTY . * Aged 20.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18510818-1760a]
1760. JOHN PRIOR , embezzling on 10th May, 3 l. 10 s.; the moneys of William Shaw, his master.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution,
WILLIAM HOOD . I am in the service of Mr. Shaw, a hay-salesman; the prisoner was also in his service. On 10th May, I sent him out with a load of hay to sell—he returned between 7 and 8 o'clock—I asked him whether be had sold it, he said he had—I asked whether he had the money—he said no, he should have it in a fortnight—on 21st June, I was at Smithfield-market, and had three loads of clover there, which I sold to a person we call Joe, for 4 l. 6 s.;—I told the prisoner to go with him, and Joe said, as soon as the clover was unloaded there would be ready tin —I said, "Do you mean money?"—he said, "Yes"—I sent the prisoner with the load, and in the evening I met the prisoner and Joe in the Old Kent-road, and asked him if he had got the money—he said he had got 3 l., that the old woman had got no more, and the old man was not at home—he gave me the 3 l.—he did not state the person's name to whom the clover was sold—he remained in the service a couple of weeks, and then left of his own accord—three or four days after he was taken into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. You then saw him in Smithfield? A. Yes; Mr. Shaw is in the habit of attending there, and selling hay— Long Joe was not present when the prisoner told me he had only got 8 l.—I had told Long Joe not to be in that cart, but be off about his business—the prisoner only sold hay on credit, when I or Mr. Shaw gave him orders so to do—I do not know if Joe is here.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Joe took the clover away, and the prisoner went with him? A. Yes; the prisoner went, to bring back the money—if he received
the 4 l. 6 s., it was his daty to bring it back to me—he brought me the 3 l., and told me that was all he had received.
The evidence given in this case having reference to another indictment, MR. GORNEY suggested that that indictment might be proceeded with, no father evidence being adduced on the present. MR. ROBINSON proposed to withdraw the present indictment. MR. GURNEY was, however, of opinion that there should be a verdict of
NOT GUILTY; the Jury could not be discharged without the consent of both parties; and, Mr. PARRY not consenting, the Jury found the prisoner
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18510818-1762]
1762. JOHN PRIOR was again indicted for embezzling, on 26th June. 2 l. 12 s. 6 d.; the moneys of William Shaw, his master.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM HOOD . On 26th June, I directed the prisoner to take half a load of bay, which came to 1 l. 17 s. 6 d., and half a load of straw, 15 s., to Mr. Parson's, at Fish-street-hill—he was to receive the money and bring it back—he did not bring the money to me—I saw him when he returned, but had not time to speak to him.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. You were very busy? A. He went away before I came back out of the office—I asked him next morning for it—he remained in our service two days after that, and was taken into custody at Smithfield, where I attend.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. What did he say the next morning? A. He told me he had lost it.
WILLIAM SHAW . The prisoner has not paid me the 2 l. 12 s. 6 d.
JOHN PARSON . I lived with John Connett, my son-in-law, a butcher of Fish-street-hill. I purchased some hay and straw in Smithfield; it was delivered at Dowgate-hill—the prisoner then came back to Fish-street-hill, and I saw him paid the money, 2 l. 12 s. 6 d.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18510818-1763]
1763. JOHN PRIOR was again indicted for embezzling, on 21st June, 1 l. 6 s.; the moneys of William Shaw, his master.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
This was the indictment to which the evidence given in the first case applied.
MR. PARRY applied to withdraw the plea of NOT GUILTY, and put in a plea of AUTREFOIS ACQUIT, which he submitted he was entitled to do at any period within six days. MR. GURNEY, after having consulted MR. JUSTICE WIGHTMAN, was of opinion that he could not receive the plea.
WILLIAM HOOD . On 21st June I sold Joe a load of clover, and sent the prisoner with him to receive the money—it was his duty when he had received it to pay it over to me—4 l. 6 s. was the amount—I next saw him in the Old Kent-road, when he paid me 3 l. for the clover, and said that was all the money he had got, the old woman had not got any more, and the old man was not at home, and he would pay it in a fortnight, when he went that way.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. How long after that did be remain in Mr. Shaw's service? A. Till 28th—it was Long Joe who bought the hay—I do not know where he is—Mr. Ostler is not Long Joe —I did not sell it to Ostler—I have dealt with Mr. Ostler before, but not with Joe.
COURT. Q. The prisoner had no authority to sell it to Mr. Ostler? A. No.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. But he was to get the money and bring it back to you? A. Yes.
MR. PARRY. Q. From Joe? A. He was either to bring back the money or the hay.
Mr. ROBINSON. Q. He was not to allow the hay to go out of his pessession unless he got the money? A. No; nothing was said about getting it from anybody else—the prisoner was not in the habit of selling hay for me, without I gave him directions—I did so occasionally, but not on this occasion Joe was not with the prisoner at first, but came afterwards—they would not tell me where they were going to take the hay.
WILLIAM OSTLER . I am a carman, at 16, Earl-street, Finsbury. On 21st June I bought a load of hay— Joe and the prisoner brought it together—I put the prisoner 4 l. 7 s.—he gave me this receipt, and put his cross to it ( produced: This was a bill headed, "Mr. Ostler, bought of Mr. Shaw," amounting to 4 l. 7 s.; settled, John Prior's mark)—I do not suppose I should hive bought it only I saw Mr. Shaw's name on the cart.
MR. GURNET being of opinion upon this evidence that the money in question was not received by the prisoner in virtue of his employment, the Jury found
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18510818-1764]
1764. JAMES RICHARDSON , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Young; with intent to steal.
MR. CAARTEEN conducted the Prosecution,
FREDERICK CHAPPLE . I am waiter at the White Hart public-house, Park-street, Southwark, which is kept by Mr. Young, it is in St. Saviour's parish. On Wednesday evening, 13th Aug., between 8 and 9 o'clock, the prisoner came and went into the bagatelle-room, which is up-stairs, and adjoins my master's bed-room—a panel of the bed-room door has been broken, and is mended with a piece of sacking, nailed over it inside, and then there is a piece of wood nailed across the sacking to keep it firm—about half-past 9, I missed the prisoner from the bagatelle-room, where I had seen him ten minutes before—I went down-stairs, spoke to Mrs. Young, and in consequence of what she said I went up-stairs again, and while going up saw the prisoner coming through the panel of the door—he came down-stairs, passed me on the stairs, and I followed him to the door—Mrs. Young had followed me op-stairs, and went into the bed-room—at the front-door the prisoner looked round', saw me, and ran away—I followed, and caught him about twenty yards from the house—he up with his fist and ran. down Fishmonger-alley—I cried, "Stop thief!" and he was stopped in the alley by Richards—when he was stopped I saw him stoop down by the side of the wall, and heard something jingle—a light was afterwards brought, and the policeman picked up two skeleton keys on the spot in my presence.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How many persons were in the bagatelle-room? A. About six—the bed-room door is at the side of the bagatelle-room door—half the panel was out—a man must kneel down to get through—I do not know in what condition the door was that evening—I know it was half-past 9 o'clock when I missed him, because there is a clock in the bar and I went down to inquire whether he had passed the bar.
ELIZA YOUNG . I am the wife of John Young, who keeps the White Hart. On 23rd Aug. Chappie came to me about twenty minutes or half-past 9 o'clock; I followed him up-stairs, and as we were going up I saw the prisoner, but did not see where he came from—I said, "Watch him! watch him!"—he was then on the same stair as me—he darted off out of the house as quick as possible, and Chappie followed him—I went up-stairs and found the aperture in my door had been opened, the wood was entirely removed, and the sacking from one side, and left on the nails on the other—the door was still locked—I had
seen it about 9, or a few minutes before, and it was quite safe then—I searched the room, but missed nothing.
JOHN ROBERT MORRIS . I am a ginger-beer manufacturer, at 13, Fishmonger-alley. On 13th Aug., about a quarter to 10 o'clock, I was at my door talking to a neighbour, and heard a cry of "Stop thief!"—I got into a corner by Mr. Watson's premises—the prisoner ran up to me, I said, "Halloo!" and he chucked something over the wall into Mr. Watson's yard—I afterwards went and spoke to Mr. Watson.
WILLIAM WATSON . I am a hat-manufacturer, at 76, Redcross-street. Mr. Morris spoke to me—I went into my yard, which adjoins Fishmonger, alley, and there found this case of picklocks ( produced).
THOMAS RICHARDS ( policeman, M 10). I was in Fishmonger-alley on 13th Aug., about 10 minutes to 10 o'clock, heard a cry of "Stop thief!" and saw the prisoner running towards me—I laid hold of him, and he said, "Let me go, you b——r"—he took his hand from his pocket, and stooped down, and I heard something jink —I called for a light, and at his feet found there two skeleton-keys ( produced)—Chappie came up, and I took the prisoner to the station—I afterwards went to the prosecutor's, and found these keys would unlock all the drawers.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Twelve Months ,
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, SEPTEMBER 15 TH.
